This study establishes a significant foundation for preparing the practical applications of Chinese medical LLMs. MedBench is publicly accessible at https://medbench.opencompass.org.cn.</p></details> | 25 pages.4 figures |
| **[Med42 -- Evaluating Fine-Tuning Strategies for Medical LLMs: Full-Parameter vs. Parameter-Efficient Approaches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.14779v1)** | 2024-04-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This study presents a comprehensive analysis and comparison of two predominant fine-tuning methodologies - full-parameter fine-tuning and parameter-efficient tuning - within the context of medical Large Language Models (LLMs). We developed and refined a series of LLMs, based on the Llama-2 architecture, specifically designed to enhance medical knowledge retrieval, reasoning, and question-answering capabilities. Our experiments systematically evaluate the effectiveness of these tuning strategies across various well-known medical benchmarks. Notably, our medical LLM Med42 showed an accuracy level of 72% on the US Medical Licensing Examination (USMLE) datasets, setting a new standard in performance for openly available medical LLMs. Through this comparative analysis, we aim to identify the most effective and efficient method for fine-tuning LLMs in the medical domain, thereby contributing significantly to the advancement of AI-driven healthcare applications.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published at AAAI 2024 Spring Symposium - Clinical Foundation Models</p></details> |
| **[Qilin-Med: Multi-stage Knowledge Injection Advanced Medical Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.09089v2)** | 2024-04-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Integrating large language models (LLMs) into healthcare holds great potential but faces challenges. Pre-training LLMs from scratch for domains like medicine is resource-heavy and often unfeasible. On the other hand, sole reliance on Supervised Fine-tuning (SFT) can result in overconfident predictions and may not tap into domain-specific insights. In response, we present a multi-stage training method combining Domain-specific Continued Pre-training (DCPT), SFT, and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). In addition, we publish a 3Gb Chinese Medicine (ChiMed) dataset, encompassing medical question answering, plain texts, knowledge graphs, and dialogues, segmented into three training stages. The medical LLM trained with our pipeline, Qilin-Med, shows substantial performance improvement. In the CPT and SFT phases, Qilin-Med achieved 38.4% and 40.0% accuracy on the CMExam test set, respectively. It outperformed the basemodel Baichuan-7B (accuracy: 33.5%), by 7.5%. In the DPO phase, it scored 16.66 in BLEU-1 and 27.44 in ROUGE-1 on the Huatuo-26M test set, bringing further improvement to the SFT phase (12.69 in BLEU-1 and 24.21 in ROUGE-1). Additionally, we have further enhanced the model's performance through the Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) approach. Experiments demonstrate that Qilin-Med-RAG achieves an accuracy rate of 42.8% on CMExam. These results highlight the contribution of our novel training approach in building LLMs for medical applications.</p></details> |  |
| **[MING-MOE: Enhancing Medical Multi-Task Learning in Large Language Models with Sparse Mixture of Low-Rank Adapter Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.09027v1)** | 2024-04-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models like ChatGPT have shown substantial progress in natural language understanding and generation, proving valuable across various disciplines, including the medical field. Despite advancements, challenges persist due to the complexity and diversity inherent in medical tasks which often require multi-task learning capabilities. Previous approaches, although beneficial, fall short in real-world applications because they necessitate task-specific annotations at inference time, limiting broader generalization. This paper introduces MING-MOE, a novel Mixture-of-Expert~(MOE)-based medical large language model designed to manage diverse and complex medical tasks without requiring task-specific annotations, thus enhancing its usability across extensive datasets. MING-MOE employs a Mixture of Low-Rank Adaptation (MoLoRA) technique, allowing for efficient parameter usage by maintaining base model parameters static while adapting through a minimal set of trainable parameters. We demonstrate that MING-MOE achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance on over 20 medical tasks, illustrating a significant improvement over existing models. This approach not only extends the capabilities of medical language models but also improves inference efficiency.</p></details> | 15 pages, 3 figures |
| **[Introducing L2M3, A Multilingual Medical Large Language Model to Advance Health Equity in Low-Resource Regions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.08705v1)** | 2024-04-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Addressing the imminent shortfall of 10 million health workers by 2030, predominantly in Low- and Middle-Income Countries (LMICs), this paper introduces an innovative approach that harnesses the power of Large Language Models (LLMs) integrated with machine translation models. This solution is engineered to meet the unique needs of Community Health Workers (CHWs), overcoming language barriers, cultural sensitivities, and the limited availability of medical dialog datasets. I have crafted a model that not only boasts superior translation capabilities but also undergoes rigorous fine-tuning on open-source datasets to ensure medical accuracy and is equipped with comprehensive safety features to counteract the risks of misinformation. Featuring a modular design, this approach is specifically structured for swift adaptation across various linguistic and cultural contexts, utilizing open-source components to significantly reduce healthcare operational costs. This strategic innovation markedly improves the accessibility and quality of healthcare services by providing CHWs with contextually appropriate medical knowledge and diagnostic tools. This paper highlights the transformative impact of this context-aware LLM, underscoring its crucial role in addressing the global healthcare workforce deficit and propelling forward healthcare outcomes in LMICs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Training A Chinese Large Language Model for Anesthesiology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.02742v1)** | 2024-03-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models (LLMs) have gained popularity recently due to their significant practical utility. However, most existing research focuses on general medicine, and there is a need for in-depth study of LLMs in specific fields like anesthesiology. To fill the gap, we introduce Hypnos, a Chinese Anesthesia model built upon existing LLMs, e.g., Llama. Hypnos' contributions have three aspects: 1) The data, such as utilizing Self-Instruct, acquired from current LLMs likely includes inaccuracies. Hypnos implements a cross-filtering strategy to improve the data quality. This strategy involves using one LLM to assess the quality of the generated data from another LLM and filtering out the data with low quality. 2) Hypnos employs a general-to-specific training strategy that starts by fine-tuning LLMs using the general medicine data and subsequently improving the fine-tuned LLMs using data specifically from Anesthesiology. The general medical data supplement the medical expertise in Anesthesiology and enhance the effectiveness of Hypnos' generation. 3) We introduce a standardized benchmark for evaluating medical LLM in Anesthesiology. Our benchmark includes both publicly available instances from the Internet and privately obtained cases from the Hospital. Hypnos outperforms other medical LLMs in anesthesiology in metrics, GPT-4, and human evaluation on the benchmark dataset.</p></details> |  |
| **[MedBench: A Large-Scale Chinese Benchmark for Evaluating Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.12806v1)** | 2023-12-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The emergence of various medical large language models (LLMs) in the medical domain has highlighted the need for unified evaluation standards, as manual evaluation of LLMs proves to be time-consuming and labor-intensive. To address this issue, we introduce MedBench, a comprehensive benchmark for the Chinese medical domain, comprising 40,041 questions sourced from authentic examination exercises and medical reports of diverse branches of medicine. In particular, this benchmark is composed of four key components: the Chinese Medical Licensing Examination, the Resident Standardization Training Examination, the Doctor In-Charge Qualification Examination, and real-world clinic cases encompassing examinations, diagnoses, and treatments. MedBench replicates the educational progression and clinical practice experiences of doctors in Mainland China, thereby establishing itself as a credible benchmark for assessing the mastery of knowledge and reasoning abilities in medical language learning models. We perform extensive experiments and conduct an in-depth analysis from diverse perspectives, which culminate in the following findings: (1) Chinese medical LLMs underperform on this benchmark, highlighting the need for significant advances in clinical knowledge and diagnostic precision. (2) Several general-domain LLMs surprisingly possess considerable medical knowledge. These findings elucidate both the capabilities and limitations of LLMs within the context of MedBench, with the ultimate goal of aiding the medical research community.</p></details> | accepted by AAAI-24 |

## Large Language Models
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[GBClaw: An agentic AI framework for atomistic grain boundary research and mechanistic discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28629v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic artificial intelligence (AI) systems hold immense potential for automating complex workflows in materials discovery and design, but extending them to domain-intensive mechanistic investigations remains challenging. Here, we present GBClaw, an agentic AI framework for investigating the structure and deformation of general grain boundaries (GBs). Although general GBs constitute the vast majority of real-world interfaces in polycrystalline materials, they remain understudied because their structural complexity and interfacial mismatch complicate experimental and modeling research. GBClaw integrates large language models (LLMs) into a modular architecture comprising an orchestrator, specialized agents, domain-specific tools, simulation interfaces, and hierarchical memory. Through dynamic replanning and iterative workflow refinement, the framework effectively connects systematic simulations with targeted mechanistic analysis. Applied to 14 general asymmetric tilt GBs in nickel, GBClaw characterized interfacial defect structures and identified diverse deformation modes across these incommensurate boundaries. Comparing boundary sliding with and without secondary GB misfit dislocations revealed substantial differences in shear strength, underscoring the critical role of defect hierarchies in sliding resistance. Furthermore, researcher-guided follow-up calculations traced an anomalously high shear resistance to metastable boundary structures. This work demonstrates that combining agentic AI with domain expertise bridges high-throughput computational screening with deep physical understanding of complex material interfaces.</p></details> |  |
| **[Semantic Observability for Autonomous Systems Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28611v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern distributed microservice deployments generate massive volumes of heterogeneous, multi-format observability logs across physically and logically partitioned infrastructure. Isolating root causes during cascading failures remains an arduous task for Site Reliability Engineers (SREs), bottlenecked by manual keyword filtering and disjointed temporal correlation across monitoring dashboards. We introduce Semantic Observability, an autonomous multi-agent framework that unifies disparate log streams into a dynamic, queryable Property Graph Database. By modeling physical infrastructure topologies, logical software dependencies, and chronological event sequences as attributed nodes and directed relations, it bridges the semantic gap inherent in flat, unstructured log repositories. Building on this unified substrate, we deploy a stateful multi-agent system orchestrated through Directed Cyclic Graphs (DCGs). The system autonomously navigates the topology, applying causal graph algorithms and temporal back-traversal to isolate the precise blast radius of anomalies. The pruned subgraph is then interpreted by a Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning engine to generate contextualized, code-level Root Cause Analyses (RCA) and actionable remediations. This paper establishes the foundational system architecture, formal relational ontology, and multi-agent control loops of Semantic Observability. We show conceptually how the framework eliminates ambient telemetry noise, reduces Mean Time to Resolution (MTTR), compresses LLM token consumption, and prevents structural hallucinations. We also specify an open-source validation framework on standard benchmarks (LogHub and Train-Ticket), laying the groundwork for a forthcoming companion paper on exhaustive quantitative evaluation and production benchmarking.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 3 figures. Foundational architecture paper; companion evaluation paper forthcoming</p></details> |
| **[InterWebCoder: Learning Interactive Webpage Generation from Executable Tests via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28610v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive webpages underpin modern applications, but reproducing both their visual appearance and multi-step behavior remains challenging for multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Scalable training data must capture user actions, state transitions, and expected outcomes, while real-world webpage code is often entangled with irrelevant scripts and external dependencies. Moreover, existing GUI-agent-based verification provides slow and potentially unstable functional feedback. To address these challenges, we propose InterWebCoder, an efficient framework for interactive webpage code generation. InterWebCoder introduces a Test-Specification-Guided and Execution-Verified Data Synthesis pipeline that records real-world interaction trajectories and synthesizes self-contained webpage code with executable test scripts. A failureattributed repair mechanism uses runtime evidence to locate and iteratively fix errors in either the webpage code or test script. We then combine test-ID-weighted supervised fine-tuning with group-relative policy optimization, directly optimizing functional correctness and visual fidelity using test pass rates and state-level visual similarity. This replaces costly GUI-agent verification with fast, reproducible executable feedback. Our pipeline produces 1,736 verified samples from 2,058 tasks, achieving an 84.35% synthesis success rate. On IWR-Bench and Vision2Web, InterWebCoder achieves final scores of 48.54 and 48.42 with a 9B backbone, outperforming 30B-level models and Gemini-3.7-Flash while remaining competitive with Claude-Sonnet-5. It reduces reward-computation latency by over 97%, eliminates external MLLM verification costs, and accelerates end-to-end training by up to 1.4x.</p></details> |  |
| **[Benchmarking Arabic Dialects for Math Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28607v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mathematical reasoning remains underexplored for low-resource languages, including Dialectal Arabic. To bridge this gap, we introduce the DAR-MGSM (Dialectal Arabic Multilingual Grade School Math) Benchmark, the first manually curated benchmark for evaluating mathematical reasoning in Dialectal Arabic. DAR-MGSM consists of math word problems (MWPs) requiring multi-step reasoning and covers five major Arabic dialects alongside Modern Standard Arabic (MSA). The benchmark comprises two parallel versions: translated-only and translated & culturally localized. The latter version adapts entities and references to regional varieties. We evaluate eleven Arabic-centric and multilingual large language models on DAR-MGSM. The results show that the performance generally declines on the localized version, highlighting the challenges introduced by culturally grounded adaptations. However, the magnitude of this effect varies across models, with Arabic-centric models exhibiting different levels of sensitivity to localization. A mechanistic interpretability analysis shows that translated and localized variants of the same MWPs often induce distinct internal reasoning trajectories, even when aggregate accuracy remains similar. Overall, our results show that mathematical reasoning performance depends on both linguistic variety and cultural context.</p></details> |  |
| **[Attention-Aligned Reasoning for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03223v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) tend to generate a long reasoning chain when solving complex tasks. However, as the reasoning chain extends, critical intermediate steps and the original prompt will be buried in the context, receiving insufficient attention and leading to errors. In this work, we present ATAR, a novel reasoning method that leverages the inherent reasoning structure to steer LLM attention. Our experiments show that ATAR outperforms SOTA methods across six benchmarks, achieving up to 15.39% absolute improvement. Furthermore, with ATAR, "non-reasoning" models achieve comparable or even better performance compared to reasoning models of the same size in most benchmarks. Finally, our ablation studies show that the attention alignment component contributes significantly, and that these improvements are persist under different attentionsteering backends.</p></details> | <details><summary>EMNLP...</summary><p>EMNLP 2026 (Findings)</p></details> |
| **[Cache Engineering for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28577v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents can handle increasingly complex tasks, navigating long trajectories of observations and actions. As these trajectories grow, key-value (KV) cache I/O and storage become the dominant cost. Trajectories can also exceed the LLM's maximum context length, requiring control over active cache growth. Prior work uses context engineering to reduce cache size by trimming or summarizing history. However, modifying the trajectory prefix invalidates subsequent cached states and triggers costly re-prefilling of retained context. Task accuracy can also suffer when too little context is retained to limit re-prefill costs. We propose cache engineering as a new scheme. It allows agents to directly control their active KV cache without modifying the trajectory or re-prefilling retained context. Specifically, agents can proactively use drop to exclude selected cache from future attention and repos to adjust retained keys' position encodings. We formalize these cache operations and identify history dependence as a serving challenge: identical token sequences can yield different cache states under different cache operation sequences, making prefix matching insufficient for correct cache reuse. Thus, we co-design the AKV serving engine. It provides a stateless interface compatible with existing serving APIs, while internally managing stateful cache reuse, eviction, and restoration. Experiments show that a tool-response rolling-drop cache policy improves average benchmark scores by 1.7 points and reduces average cost by 27.7%, compared with conventional threshold-triggered summarization. With AKV, it achieves up to 1.2x the output throughput and a 1.5x speedup in P95 request latency. Recursive self-improvement of cache policies further reduces cost by 7.1% over expert design. Our code is at https://github.com/thu-nics/AKV.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Token-Energy Law of LLM Decode: Active Bytes and Realized Time Account for Joules per Token](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28576v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Energy-efficient deployment of large language models requires understanding how model architecture, hardware, and serving settings shape energy per generated token. We introduce the Token-Energy Law, a compact, interpretable model of GPU-board energy per output token for dense, full-history decoding. It relates token energy to two core dimensions: active bytes and realized decode time. Active bytes describe logical byte demand per output, separating dense weights shared within a decode iteration from private key--value (KV) histories read and updated for each request. Realized decode time is the elapsed decode duration divided by the number of output tokens produced. We combine the two byte coordinates and time in a common affine form, with coefficients calibrated for each model and hardware/runtime configuration. This formulation explains how batching amortizes shared-weight work, longer attended histories increase private KV demand, and throughput determines the time-associated cost per output. Our experiments cover three models under BF16 decoding on NVIDIA Hopper and Blackwell GPUs, including two-GPU tensor parallelism on Hopper. Using fixed coefficients and observed decode time, held-out condition-mean energy estimates differ from measurements by at most 3.73%.</p></details> |  |
| **[When Do LLMs Help with Feature Selection? A Systematic Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28556v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can leverage semantic feature names alongside summary statistics for tabular feature selection, yet it remains unclear when such information improves subset selection relative to established statistical methods. We investigate this question within an 18-dataset framework comprising 10 datasets for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) training, 2 for checkpoint validation, and 6 held-out evaluation datasets. We compare five classical baselines, frozen Qwen2.5-7B and Llama-3.3-70B selectors, and two GRPO-adapted Qwen2.5-7B models, reporting classification ROC-AUC and regression R^2 separately and using dataset-wise ranks for cross-task comparison. Across five seeded evaluation partitions, classical selectors generally achieve higher predictive performance than the tested 7B LLM/RL selectors, including under a pre-specified ~33% feature budget with strict train-only classical fitting, while the 7B selectors retain compact subsets. A cardinality-matched semantic sensitivity experiment shows that, for Zero-shot Qwen-7B, informative feature names improve regression performance over anonymized and shuffled alternatives (+0.1964 and +0.1606 mean R^2), whereas aggregate classification differences are smaller (+0.0159 and +0.0084 ROC-AUC) and dataset-dependent. These results characterize when semantic information benefits LLM-based feature selection and show that classical selectors remain strong baselines for predictive tabular feature selection.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 6 figures, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[Decomposing Refusal Steering in Mixture-of-Experts Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04160v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safety alignment in instruction-tuned large language models (LLMs) depends on a model's ability to reliably refuse harmful or disallowed requests. Recent work has shown that a steering vector can be applied to a dense LLM during inference to suppress refusal behavior and induce responses to harmful requests. We extend this refusal steering method to three open-source Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs to decompose and better understand how refusal mechanisms operate across MoE components. We find that steering performance is uninhibited by the complex routing patterns inherent to the MoE architecture, and that a single expert recovers 78% of the full steering effect on average when free to select its own location. However, expert-level steering only recovers 54% on average when constrained to the location used by full-layer steering, a gap that widens further under safety-related system prompts. Our results also show that refusal signals captured by these steering methods differ from those captured by expert routing behavior. Together, these results suggest that refusal behavior is distributed unevenly across the MoE LLM architecture rather than governed by a single unified mechanism.</p></details> |  |
| **[Randomized YaRN Improves Length Generalization for Long-Context Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23687v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are typically pretrained on short sequences and then extended to work on longer sequences with additional training. However, such LLMs still struggle to further generalize to very long sequences. We propose Randomized YaRN, a training method that improves length generalization by combining YaRN-based positional extrapolation with randomized positional encoding and a length curriculum. During training on short context data, tokens are assigned YaRN positional encodings sampled from a larger position range, exposing the model to out-of-distribution positional representations even on short-context inputs. We evaluate Randomized YaRN on three challenging long-context reasoning benchmarks, BABILong, Multi-Round Coreference Resolution (MRCR), and LongBench v2. When training on data with short context, Randomized YaRN consistently improves reasoning performance on context lengths from 16K to 128K and outperforms standard fine-tuning, with the largest gains appearing at far out-of-distribution lengths. Our results suggest that progressively exposing models to OOD positional distributions provides an effective recipe for generalizable long-context reasoning.</p></details> | EMNLP 2026 Findings |
| **[BitMTP: When Multi-Token Prediction Meets Low-Bit Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28500v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For efficient deployment of large language models (LLMs), methods of ultra-low-bit backbones reduce weight storage, memory traffic, and linear-layer execution cost, while multi-token prediction (MTP) methods reduce sequential decoding steps by predicting multiple future tokens in parallel. Although complementary, the direct combination of these methods cannot accelerate decoding at comparable quality: masked drafting lacks intermediate token input supplied in next-token training, while wider computation and causal key-value (KV) updates can offset the savings from fewer steps. To address these challenges, we propose BitMTP, an algorithm-system co-design framework for efficient MTP on ultra-low-bit LLM backbones. A joint next-token prediction and MTP objective equips a single model with both autoregressive (AR) generation and block-causal drafting. Confidence-only decoding, speculative-only decoding, and hybrid decoding provide different quality-throughput operating points without using separate draft and verifier models. Accepted-prefix alignment keeps the committed prefix, materialized causal KV boundary, and CUDA Graph replay state consistent for continued generation. BitMTP uses this contract to select width-specialized low-bit execution and reuse captured graphs over shared KV storage. On NVIDIA H20, BitMTP with hybrid decoding achieves five-task mean speedups of 2.2x on BitNet b1.58-2B and 2.1-2.4x on 1.7B-4B Bonsai models at comparable task quality. On RTX 3090 and Jetson Orin, it further achieves up to 2.0x in five-task mean throughput and 3.3x on MBPP+, respectively. These results demonstrate that BitMTP accelerates ultra-low-bit LLM generation across backbones, model sizes, and GPU types. The source code is released at https://github.com/PolyU-VCLab/BitMTP</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 16 pages, 3 figures. Code: https://github.com/PolyU-VCLab/BitMTP</p></details> |
| **[Knowledge Acquisition During Pre-training? Large Language Models Learn Better With Auxiliary Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04180v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Gaps remain in our understanding of how large language models (LLMs) acquire knowledge during pre-training. We find that auxiliary views, conceptual reformulations, are key to this process. We design controlled experiments to isolate this. First, holding the token budget fixed, allocating tokens from paraphrases to auxiliary views improves learning even for factual recall, which we use near-verbatim sentences from the original document to test. Second, the effectiveness of auxiliary views is not contingent on the strength of the model that generates them. Third, we establish that paraphrasing is helpful but only at smaller batch sizes. Fourth, we identify types of knowledge that aid learning in the presence of prior knowledge gaps. Finally, we examine how these effects manifest mechanistically via layer-wise biases and compression. Together, our findings suggest that auxiliary representations of knowledge are a key factor in the success of pre-training, and explains why data diversity matters. Further, our results provide clear recipes for synthetic data augmentation along with implications on the self-improvement in LLMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Findings of EMNLP 2026; Updated with refined writing, new figures, corrected tables and plots</p></details> |
| **[The Trilemma of Truth in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.23921v6)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The public often attributes human-like qualities to large language models (LLMs), assuming that they "know" certain things. In reality, LLMs encode information retained during training as internal probabilistic knowledge. This study examines existing methods for probing the veracity of that knowledge and identifies three flawed underlying assumptions. To address these flaws, we introduce sAwMIL (Sparse-Aware Multiple-Instance Learning), a multiclass probing framework that combines multiple-instance learning with conformal prediction. sAwMIL leverages LLMs' internal representations to classify statements as true, false, or neither. We evaluate sAwMIL across 16 open-source LLMs, including default and chat-based variants, using three new curated datasets. Our results show that (1) common probing methods fail to provide a reliable and transferable veracity direction and, in some settings, perform worse than zero-shot prompting; (2) truth and falsehood are not encoded symmetrically; and (3) LLMs encode a third type of signal that is distinct from both true and false.</p></details> | <details><summary>The m...</summary><p>The main text is 10 pages long (plus 3 pages of references); supplementary material (62 pages) is included in the same PDF</p></details> |
| **[Foundational Refinement Proofs for EVM Bytecode, at the Price of Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26306v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Relating executable code to a high-level model of its behavior has been a central concern of programming-language research for decades. Formally verified compilers, translation validators and certifying compilers address this problem through different trade-offs between proof effort, automation, generality, and the size of the trusted base. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have begun to change the economics of formal verification. Agentic proof development is now capable of producing machine-checked proofs, sometimes unsupervised, at a scale and speed that were previously out of reach. We propose a verification architecture that takes advantage of this shift. An executable obtains a machine-checked proof that it behaves according to a high-level model, independently of the compiler that produced it. The proof is written by an LLM agent. We evaluate the feasibility of this architecture for the Ethereum Virtual Machine (EVM). We build EquiVM, a foundational framework in Lean comprising an executable EVM semantics and a formal specification language, Stele, that gives a high-level authoritative model of the bytecode's behavior, but commits to no source language or compilation toolchain. We state what it means for EVM bytecode to refine a Stele spec and we build a proof library to facilitate proof construction. We evaluate the capabilities of frontier LLMs to construct such proofs, as well as the cost of proof production and repair. Our results show that agents prove 35 real production contracts end to end, without modifying their source or bytecode, among them core contracts of Safe, Morpho, Compound and Uniswap protocols, with bytecode of size close to the maximum the EVM allows. Several proofs need only the initial prompt, the rest a handful of human inputs, and existing proofs are repaired in a fraction of the original time when the source or the compiler configuration changes.</p></details> |  |
| **[Stratified Sparse Attention: Reducing the Sparse-to-Dense Gap at Extreme Sparsity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28441v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model inference over long contexts is increasingly bottlenecked by the cost of attending to a growing Key-Value (KV) cache. Sparse attention reduces this cost by accessing only a fraction of the cache, but existing methods face a trade-off between deterministic retrieval, which captures high-scoring tokens but discards residual attention mass, and sampling, which improves coverage at the cost of higher variance. Hybrid methods combine top-$k$ retrieval with uniform sampling, yet still exhibit a substantial gap from dense attention at aggressive sparsity. We introduce \emph{Stratified Sparse Attention (SSA)}, a unified framework combining deterministic retrieval with score-guided sampling. SSA deterministically attends to high-scoring tokens while sampling the residual using proxy attention scores and correcting for their inclusion probabilities, thereby recovering attention mass beyond the retrieval set. SSA encompasses top-$k$, top-$p$, and hybrid top-$k$+sampling as special cases. Across RULER, LongBench, LOFT, and AIME, and five models ranging from 3B to 27B parameters, SSA consistently improves over the baselines at matched sparsity. On RULER-HARD with Ministral-3-8B, SSA improves accuracy over the strongest practical baseline from 81.8\% to 84.2\% at 32K and from 63.8\% to 69.3\% at 128K under $50\times$ attention sparsity, recovering 94\% and 85\% of dense-attention performance, respectively. With optimized CUDA kernels, SSA achieves up to $2.96\times$ decode speedup over FlashInfer at 1M context, demonstrating that its accuracy gains translate into practical long-context inference acceleration. We open-source our code at https://github.com/skylight-org/SSA-Paper-Code.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages, 5 figures, 13 tables</p></details> |
| **[The Labor of Storytelling: How People with Chronic Illnesses Leverage AI and Communities for Narrative Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28438v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The distinctiveness of people with chronic illness as storytellers is how they experience their illness. Symptoms, pain, and biological disruptions are eloquent forms of speech of the body that beget social articulation. The stories people tell about illness shape how suffering is legitimized, and acted upon. Large language models have emerged as new mediators within this landscape---a role once predominantly supported by communities. In doing so, these systems participate in what we term narrative translation: transforming lived illness experiences for particular audiences and purposes. Through 21 prototype-driven semi-structured interviews with people with chronic illness, we contribute an empirical account of how participants navigate this mediation alongside existing sources of support. Our findings surface distinct yet complementary roles participants envisioned for AI and their communities, and how trust in AI was shaped by learning model-boundaries and perceiving understanding. | **[Every Model Leaves a Trace: Multi-Resolution LLM Fingerprinting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28434v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models leave model-specific traces in their generated text, but it remains unclear how finely these fingerprints can distinguish related models and which signals remain informative as obvious cues are reduced. | **[A Digital Twin to Improve Cyber-Social Impact](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28430v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The increasing complexity of Industrial Control Systems (ICS) and their integration with IT infrastructures has significantly expanded the cyber threat landscape. (2) Continuous Engagement evaluates sustained participation through continuous visual tracking and visually grounded query interaction. | **[CountVideoBench: A Benchmark to Evaluate Prediction Biases on the Object Counting task by Video-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28356v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Object counting in videos remains a poorly characterized capability of multimodal large language models. | **[SoK: Grading Open-Text Legal Answers, from Law School Exams to Trustworthy Rubric Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19992v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Law students are examined through open-text answers: case solutions, essays and memos that apply the law to a set of facts. The loop reinforces itself, and removing the agent's mentions of an attribute lowers its later disclosure about twice as much as single-turn effects predict. Users cannot leave it on their own, since correcting a value discloses the attribute, declining to discuss it shifts later predictions toward sensitive values, and incorrect values left in place become entrenched. | **[From Outcomes to Components: A Counterfactual Evaluation Framework for LLM-Proposed Repairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26650v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A repair action proposed by a large language model (LLM) specifies which records to change and how to change their values. A failed action may still contain a useful choice of records or a useful update, depending on how the two are paired. We introduce a counterfactual evaluation framework that separates selected records (targets) from the applied change (an update). Four executions combine proposed and reference targets and updates to measure the effects of correcting or retaining each component and their interaction. We evaluate the framework on two tasks: traffic assignment, where affected demand must be identified from road counts, and database repair, where affected rows must be identified from aggregate reports. An injected fault supplies a reference action that reverses it, and the recovery rate measures an action's loss reduction relative to that reference. For sparse-target actions in the traffic experiments, retaining the proposed update on reference targets achieves 79.4% of the reference loss reduction, compared with 43.7% from tuning update strength on the proposed targets. In the database experiments, update replacement worsens 17 of 108 actions, all selecting unaffected records, with sharply different frequencies across models. The value of a proposed update depends on the targets it is paired with. Missed targets hide useful updates, while excess targets can make a milder update better than the reference correction. In a component-reuse study, testing new target-update pairings from two proposals raises mean held-out recovery rate to 0.616, compared with 0.580 when selecting between the original proposals.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, 5 figures, 15 tables. Code and data to be released with the camera-ready version</p></details> |
| **[DECODEM: Data Extraction from Corporate Organizational Documents via Enhanced Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15879v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Much empirical legal research depends on translating unstructured text into structured variables. In corporate governance research as elsewhere, this translation has traditionally been performed by humans. Manual coding is time-consuming and costly. In corporate governance research, standardized commercial datasets offer an alternative, but they cover a limited set of firms and governance variables. This paper introduces the DECODEM Benchmarks and uses them to evaluate whether large language models can extract corporate governance variables from charters and bylaws. The benchmarks combine a sample of corporate charters and bylaws of publicly traded U.S. corporations obtained from the SEC's EDGAR database with hand-coded labels that capture whether a document includes a specific governance arrangement. The paper uses these benchmarks to evaluate a range of extraction approaches and models. The approaches vary along several dimensions, including the amount of guidance included in the prompts and the extent to which the model is provided with the full document or with relevant excerpts only. The results show that current models can recover many governance variables with high accuracy across extraction approaches. At the same time, performance varies substantially across documents and variables. In particular, extraction is less accurate for charters than for bylaws, including when the comparison is limited to variables that appear in both benchmarks. The results also show that frontier models perform comparably on the tasks evaluated here and that more elaborate extraction pipelines do not consistently improve their performance. More elaborate extraction strategies matter more for efficiency-oriented models, where they can substantially narrow the performance gap relative to frontier models.</p></details> |  |
| **[MaD-RSI: Model-as-Data for Recursive Self-Improvement of Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28201v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recursive self-improvement (RSI) of agent harnesses has emerged as an effective approach to enhancing the capabilities of large language model (LLM) agents. By searching the harness space for a specific LLM on a given dataset, RSI can discover a harness tailored to the capabilities and behavioral characteristics of that model. However, such optimization often creates a strong coupling between the learned harness and its target LLM, limiting generalization across models and requiring substantial computational resources to adapt to new LLMs. Moreover, existing RSI methods typically optimize a harness using feedback from a single model and evaluate its generalization to other models only after training. This paradigm treats model differences primarily as a criterion for evaluating generalization rather than as valuable evidence for optimization. In this paper, we introduce a new perspective that treats models as data and places the harness at the center of optimization. Building on this perspective, we propose \textsc{MaD-RSI}, a framework that directly optimizes a shared harness across multiple heterogeneous LLMs by incorporating their diverse failure trajectories into the training process. Rather than assessing model differences only after optimization, \textsc{MaD-RSI} leverages these differences as training evidence to guide the self-improvement of a shared harness. Furthermore, we introduce a new evaluation framework with two complementary metrics designed to assess the effectiveness of harness optimization across models. Experiments across multiple datasets and LLMs demonstrate that \textsc{MaD-RSI} improves generalization over existing approaches while reducing training costs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment for LLM Reasoning over Temporal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28176v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) often struggle with temporally grounded reasoning because their static parametric memory is misaligned with the dynamic evolution of real-world knowledge. Although RAG and Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) provide practical ways to enhance LLMs, they remain insufficient for temporal reasoning. Existing retrieval methods typically prioritize semantic relevance while overlooking temporal validity, event order, and evolutionary dependencies, resulting in evidence that is relevant in isolation but temporally fragmented. Meanwhile, conventional PEFT methods usually employ fixed and input-agnostic low-rank updates, making it difficult to capture time-dependent entity semantics and relation drift. To address these limitations, we propose Temporal Evidence-Parameter Co-Alignment (TEPCA), a framework for LLM reasoning over TKGs. TEPCA aligns LLM reasoning with temporal knowledge at both the evidence and parameter levels. At the evidence level, it introduces time-constrained Dynamic Relevance-Guided Path (DRGP) pruning to preserve globally coherent temporal paths, together with Mutual-Information (MI)-based pruning to retain query-informative local facts under temporal constraints. At the parameter level, it integrates Dynamic-Gated LoRA (DG-LoRA), a timestamp-conditioned low-rank adaptation mechanism that dynamically modulates the effective adaptation subspace according to temporal context. This design enables LLMs to better capture fine-grained entity evolution and time-dependent relation semantics while preserving parameter efficiency. Experimental results on two TKG benchmarks demonstrate that TEPCA achieves state-of-the-art performance. Additional evaluations of DG-LoRA on eight commonsense QA datasets further verify its general input-adaptive capacity, scalability across LLM backbones, and interpretability.</p></details> |  |
| **[Africa-Papers: A 10-Billion-Token Multi-Domain Academic Corpus for African Foundation Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28173v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) depend fundamentally on the diversity, depth, and factual rigor of their pre-training corpora. However, contemporary foundational pre-training datasets (such as The Pile, S2ORC, and Dolma) exhibit a severe structural omission: African scientific, medical, legal, and agricultural scholarship constitutes less than 0.5% of global pre-training tokens. Consequently, state-of-the-art models suffer from systematic failure modes when reasoning about tropical epidemiology, localized smallholder agro-ecologies, regional economic governance, and indigenous linguistic structures. To resolve this representation deficit and advance African intellectual sovereignty in artificial intelligence, we introduce Africa-Papers, the largest curated multi-domain Pan-African academic pre-training corpus to date. Comprising over 844,807 full-text papers spanning 10.15 billion tokens across 879 Parquet shards, Africa-Papers unifies four primary scientific streams: (1) continental peer-reviewed journals indexed in DOAJ across 31 African nations, (2) the CGIAR CGSpace agricultural repository, (3) African-authored and African-focused STEM/AI preprints on arXiv, and (4) open-access international publications by scholars affiliated with African institutions indexed via OpenAlex. The entire corpus is processed into layout-aware, clean Markdown preserving section hierarchies, mathematical equations, and tabular data, accompanied by exact BPE token counts and a standardized 8-domain taxonomy. We release Africa-Papers openly on the Hugging Face Hub under the AfriSpeech organization to accelerate continual pre-training and domain evaluation for foundation models globally.</p></details> |  |
| **[Dynamic Cross-Modal Prompt Generation for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10765v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance through instruction tuning, yet real-world deployment often requires continual capability expansion across sequential tasks. In such scenarios, Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT) aims to acquire new capabilities while limiting catastrophic forgetting. Existing methods mainly follow a module-composition paradigm: they maintain task-level prompts or LoRA experts and dynamically route or aggregate a subset of them at inference. However, samples within the same task can still differ substantially in visual scenes, question intents, and reasoning demands. This motivates instance-level adaptation to individual query-image pairs rather than only selecting or combining task-level modules. To this end, we propose DRAPE (Dynamic Cross-Modal Prompt Generation), a prompt-learning framework that synthesizes continuous instance-specific soft prompts for MCIT. Instead of selecting prompts from a fixed pool, DRAPE derives prompt queries from the textual instruction and cross-attends to visual patch features, producing prompts conditioned on query-image pairs that are prepended to the frozen LLM. To mitigate forgetting during sequential updates, DRAPE applies null-space gradient projection to the shared projector and uses CLIP-based prototype routing for task-label-free generator selection at inference. Extensive experiments on MCIT benchmarks show that DRAPE achieves state-of-the-art performance among representative prompt-based and LoRA-based continual-learning baselines. Code is available at: https://github.com/LAMDA-CL/NeurIPS2026-Drape.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurlPS 2026. Code is available at https://github.com/LAMDA-CL/NeurIPS2026-Drape</p></details> |
| **[From Code Generator to Engineering Companion: A Plant-aware Agent for PLC Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28160v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Programmable Logic Controller (PLC) coding remains a manual, IDE-centric task that demands constant context switching between documentation, project files, and code editors. Recent work has demonstrated that retrieval-augmented large language models can generate vendor-compliant Structured Text at high compilation rates \cite{pred}, yet these systems treat code generation as a one-shot pipeline: the engineer submits a query and receives code, with no persistent knowledge of the specific plant. We present a plant-aware conversational copilot that extends this paradigm in three directions: plant-specific context ingestion, a chat-first interaction model for explanation and debugging, and a graduated tool-access ladder from chat to deployment. The system is designed for real plant infrastructure and keeps the engineer in control of each step that produces real-world effects.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgenTest: Mining Business Rules to Test LLM Agents for Business Logic Bugs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28116v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute business operations through tools and interact directly with application data and services. Beyond completing user requests, these agents must obey domain-specific business rules that constrain when actions are permitted and what data or resources may be affected. Violating such rules can lead to business logic bugs, even when a task appears successful. Detecting these bugs is difficult because the rules are rarely available as explicit, testable specifications. They are distributed across system prompts and tool definitions, while others remain implicit in domain knowledge. Existing agent testing approaches typically evaluate task success, predefined policies, or known attack scenarios. This limits their ability to systematically uncover previously unspecified business-rule violations. We present AgenTest, which mines business rules from system prompts, tool definitions, and domain knowledge, refines them into Given-When-Then specifications, and systematically tests each specification. For each extracted specification, AgenTest instantiates its Given conditions by constructing a satisfying environment state and simulated user, exercises the agent through multi-turn interactions, and checks its Then conditions over messages, tool calls, and state changes using a hybrid oracle. On the airline, retail, and telecom agents of tau2-bench with two underlying models, AgenTest produces 551 refined specifications, and detects violations of 89 and 121 distinct specifications, respectively -- 3.4X and 2.5X as many as all baselines combined. Only AgenTest detects cases where an agent improperly accesses or modifies another user's data. Moreover, about 60% of bug-triggering runs cannot be detected from the final state alone, highlighting the need to check the full interaction against explicit business rules.</p></details> |  |
| **[Reading Too Much Into It? Interpretive Preferences and Commitment in LLM Mentalisation under Ambiguity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28105v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used for interpersonal advice, potentially shaping how users interpret ambiguous social encounters. Existing Theory of Mind evaluations typically rely on predefined answers, while uncertainty research overlooks LLMs' preferred explanations and confidence under psychological distress. To address these gaps, we introduce MentalLoom, a novel evaluation of LLM mentalisation comprising 604 ambiguous social episodes, matched depression and social anxiety disclosures, and four-turn dialogues with unchanged event evidence. Across eight LLMs, disclosures initially elicited more negative explanations overall but more positive narrator-directed interpretations. Scripted repeated questioning showed greater emotional support, stronger positive narrator-directed endorsement, and higher certainty without new event evidence. These findings show that user context can reshape interpretive preferences and commitment while the underlying ambiguity remains unresolved.</p></details> |  |
| **[Efficient and Adaptable Detection of Malicious LLM Prompts via Bootstrap Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08062v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in natural language understanding. However, they remain susceptible to malicious prompts that induce unsafe or policy-violating behavior. Existing defenses in black-box moderation APIs offer limited transparency and adapt poorly to evolving threats, while white-box approaches using large LLM judges impose prohibitive computational costs and require expensive retraining for new attacks. We present BAGEL (Bootstrap AGgregated Ensemble Layer), a modular, lightweight, and incrementally updatable framework for malicious prompt detection. BAGEL employs a bootstrap aggregation and mixture-of-experts-inspired ensemble of finetuned models, each specialized on a different attack dataset. At inference, BAGEL aggregates predictions from a stochastically selected subset of ensemble members, optionally augmented by a lightweight learned router that predicts the most suitable member to include. When new attacks emerge, BAGEL updates incrementally by finetuning just a single small classifier (86M parameters) and adding it to the ensemble. We evaluate BAGEL on a held-out, source-disjoint set constructed from datasets never used for training or calibration, ensuring that performance reflects generalization to out-of-distribution attacks. In single-turn evaluation, BAGEL achieves an F1 score of 0.757 at a size equivalent to 430M parameters, improving to 0.772 when augmented with the router using fewer ensemble members (324M parameters). These results outperform popular black-box moderation APIs and most white-box baselines at lower costs. In multi-turn evaluation, BAGEL achieves an F1 score of 0.660, outperforming billion-parameter methods. Our results show ensembles of small finetuned classifiers can match or exceed the performance of billion-parameter guardrails while offering the adaptability and efficiency required for production systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[BAIT: Can LLMs Find What Biases a Reader? A Benchmark for Cognitive Bias Triggers in News](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28092v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Persuasive writing exploits cognitive biases, recruiting a reader's mental shortcuts to shape belief, often without the reader noticing. We call the linguistic features exploiting such mental shortcuts cognitive bias triggers. As large language models (LLMs) increasingly mediate how people encounter news, summarizing it, ranking it, and answering questions over it, a natural question is whether they can recognize the features of a text that bias a human reader. We introduce BAIT (Bias Annotation In Text), a resource for detecting cognitive bias triggers in news. BAIT pairs a schema of nine triggers, each specified in an annotator codebook, with 50 English news articles annotated by trained annotators at the article and span levels and adjudicated into a gold standard. On this data we define two tasks, detecting which triggers a text contains and locating the spans that carry them. We then benchmark six frontier LLMs zero-shot and with three or ten in-context examples. The models approach human agreement on which triggers an article contains, but rarely recover its full set of triggers, and they reach less than half of human agreement in locating the passages that carry them. Moreover, they over-predict triggers that come with easy surface cues, and most of the passages they mark for these triggers are not triggers, yet carry the trigger's surface cue far more often than random text. These findings suggest that the evaluated LLMs respond to surface cues rather than to the mechanisms that make a passage manipulative. We release the schema, the codebook, standoff annotations, and the evaluation pipeline.</p></details> |  |
| **[WildAIGC-Bench: Can AI Image Detectors Handle the Wild? A Benchmark of Generated and Edited Images in Online Circulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28087v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI-generated images linked to news events and online claims can mislead audiences, enable fraud, or cause public controversy. We introduce WildAIGC-Bench, built through recurring collection across news websites, fact-checking sources, and related social media content. The pipeline combines broad coverage, timely updates, and human-verified labels through scheduled discovery, automatic screening and preliminary annotation, and human review of image-specific evidence. The benchmark contains 407 verified AI-generated or edited images, each accompanied by a source webpage link and an evidence summary. Images retain the conditions encountered online, including compression, screenshots, and recapture. Evaluation of dedicated visual detectors, multimodal large language models, and a simple Perception Encoder baseline reveals substantial recall gaps relative to existing benchmarks. WildAIGC-Bench provides both a practical detection challenge and a basis for continued data expansion and source-based verification. The dataset is released at https://github.com/YuZijian/WildAIGC-Bench.</p></details> | 19 pages |
| **[MuPACE: Learning to Pace Muon for LoRA Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28072v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning rate schedules are typically fixed before training begins, yet when fine-tuning large language models with low-rank adapters (LoRA), the appropriate scale varies substantially with the model, task, and adapter configuration. Practitioners re-tune a schedule for every new problem, yet a tuned schedule still cannot react to the training dynamics it encounters. We instead amortize learning rate selection across LoRA fine-tuning problems. We leave the base optimizer, Muon, untouched, so that its normalized update fixes the direction, and meta-learn only the global step size. Our method MuPACE is a small recurrent policy, meta-trained across a distribution of fine-tuning runs, that conditions on the observed trajectory and emits bounded adjustments to an interpretable analytic schedule. On 64 fine-tuning problems, MuPACE outperforms standard fixed schedules at a strong shared default on 64-78% of problems and is on par with the same schedules with per-task tuned peaks. Meta-trained only on models with up to 16 layers, MuPACE transfers one-shot to unseen tasks on models as deep as 64 layers, such as Qwen3-32B and Qwen3.8-27B, where a single run remains competitive with swept schedules.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[TrajEvolve: Trajectory Evolution for Reinforcement Learning with Hindsight Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28060v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Reinforcement Learning (RL) paradigm has achieved remarkable success in enhancing the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, when applied to long-horizon tasks, it faces severe challenges: i) Due to the high task complexity, the model struggles to sample enough successful trajectories, resulting in slow convergence. ii) Relying solely on sparse final rewards causes all steps to be updated indiscriminately, encouraging redundant step generation. When both the quantity and quality of successful trajectories become difficult to guarantee, the performance is severely constrained. This dilemma stems from the limitations: existing RL paradigms rely entirely on the reasoning capability of base models, lacking the ability to actively optimize trajectories. To address the above issues, we propose a novel trajectory evolution paradigm with hindsight credit assignment, termed TrajEvolve. TrajEvolve not only relies on the trajectory inferred by the model but also generates ``evolutionary trajectories'' by refining low-quality steps in historical trajectories, thereby exposing the policy to high-quality compositions that lie within the base model's support but are rarely sampled within practical training budgets. Furthermore, TrajEvolve reformulates step-level continuous credit assignment as a binary classification task, whether each step is necessary for task completion. This simplification not only reduces the learning difficulty but also enables step labels to be obtained through counterfactual verification, achieving precise identification of redundant steps. Extensive experiments on two datasets demonstrate the superiority of the proposed method.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 accepted paper</p></details> |
| **[Poisoning-Based Backdoors Are Not Cryptographic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28045v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To defend against backdoor attacks on neural networks, the defender must identify the verifier function that determines whether an input is a trigger, rather than rejecting individual triggers in isolation, since an attacker can generate new triggers satisfying the same verifier faster than they can be blocked. If a backdoor verifier could be installed with the asymmetry of a cryptographic authentication scheme, in which only the attacker can produce trigger inputs while the defender cannot reverse-engineer the verifier from observations, learning-based defense would be impossible in principle. We show that no such asymmetry can arise from poisoning-based installation, in which the only verifier-dependent input to training is a poisoned dataset. The argument is a reduction. A training procedure that installs a verifier from poisoned data, so that the backdoor fires on unseen triggers while benign behavior is preserved, is itself a learner for that verifier. Any verifier that is computationally hard to learn is therefore impossible to install through poisoning, and any verifier that poisoning can install is reconstructible by a white-box defender holding comparably many labeled trigger observations. A complementary argument shows that a secret key supplied to the model at inference is observable to the same defender. The result takes representative trigger observations as given and concerns when the backdoor fires rather than what it does. Experiments on instruction-tuned large language models confirm the dichotomy, with no configuration yielding a backdoor that is both effective for the attacker and irrecoverable for the defender.</p></details> | NeurIPS 2026 |
| **[Ethical LLM-Assisted Research: A Framework for Responsible Delegation, Verification, and Epistemic Value](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23644v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are becoming routine instruments of scientific research, assisting with literature synthesis, hypothesis development, coding, and formal reasoning. Their use raises a central epistemic question: when parts of scientific reasoning are delegated to an artificial system, what conditions must remain under human control for the resulting knowledge claims to retain epistemic legitimacy and accountable authorship? This paper develops a normative and conceptual framework for analyzing such delegation. Scientific reasoning is treated as a distributed process in which the origin of a contribution may vary between human and machine, while responsibility for its acceptance into the scientific record remains human. The framework distinguishes content origin $O(g)$, completion of human verification $V(g)$, responsibility assignment $R(g)$, accountable human ownership $M(g)$, and epistemic outcome $E(g)$. These constructs separate the provenance of a claim from the process by which it is checked, the epistemic outcome of that checking, and the human responsibility attached to its disposition. The central proposition is that the ethical boundary of LLM-assisted research is determined primarily by adequate verification and accountable human ownership rather than by the degree of machine involvement itself. On this basis, the paper develops the notion of an \emph{epistemic audit}: a structured record of delegation, verification, provenance, and responsibility intended to make AI-assisted reasoning transparent and reviewable. The resulting framework provides a formal vocabulary for distinguishing responsible cognitive delegation from the transfer or neglect of epistemic responsibility in scientific research.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This is a substantially revised version of submit/7664457. I have extensively revised the manuscript to clarify its scholarly contribution, strengthen the formal framework and literature grounding, and remove or reformulate claims that were not sufficiently supported</p></details> |
| **[VRPAgent: LLM-Driven Discovery of Heuristic Operators for Vehicle Routing Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07073v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing high-performing heuristics for vehicle routing problems (VRPs) is a complex task that requires both intuition and deep domain knowledge. Large language model (LLM)-based code generation has recently shown promise across many domains, but it still falls short of producing heuristics that rival those crafted by human experts. In this paper, we propose VRPAgent, a framework for VRPs that integrates LLM-generated components into a metaheuristic and refines them through a novel genetic search. By using the LLM to generate problem-specific operators, embedded within a shared metaheuristic framework for VRPs, VRPAgent keeps tasks manageable, guarantees correctness, and still enables the discovery of novel and powerful strategies. Across multiple VRPs, including the capacitated VRP, the VRP with time windows, and the prize-collecting VRP, our method discovers heuristic operators that outperform handcrafted methods and recent learning-based approaches while requiring only a single CPU core. To our knowledge, VRPAgent is one of the first LLM-based methods to advance the state-of-the-art in VRPs, highlighting a promising future for automated heuristics discovery.</p></details> | Published in TMLR |
| **[RANGER: Retrieval Agent for Navigating Graphs with Semantic Evaluation Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28010v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of Large Language Models and Reinforcement Learning offers a promising direction for complex sequential decision-making tasks, such as multi-hop question answering over knowledge graphs. Conversely, RL agents excel at learning navigation policies but are hindered by the difficulty of designing semantically meaningful reward functions. This paper introduces a novel hybrid approach in which an LLM serves as a mentor to an RL agent during training by providing dense, semantically informed reward signals. The RL agent, built on a Transformer-based policy and trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization, learns to navigate a knowledge graph to retrieve relevant information. The LLM evaluates the semantic relevance of the agent's traversed paths, generating reward signals that align the agent's behavior with the information needs expressed in a query. We integrate this Retrieval Agent for Navigating Graphs with Semantic Evaluation Rewards (RANGER) into a complete question-answering pipeline and evaluate it on the open question answering datasets. Experimental results show that the proposed approach outperforms both a baseline retrieval-augmented system with classical graph search and one using an RL agent trained with heuristic rewards, achieving a significant increase in NLP metrics and LLM-as-a-Judge scores. Our findings demonstrate that deep integration of LLMs into the RL training loop yields more effective and semantically aligned retrieval policies for complex reasoning tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 8 figures, 3 tables. Includes appendices</p></details> |
| **[ArtSeek: Deep artwork understanding via multimodal in-context reasoning and late interaction retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.21917v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Analyzing digitized artworks requires both visual interpretation and deep artistic, contextual, and historical knowledge. We introduce ArtSeek, a multimodal framework for art analysis that combines multimodal large language models with retrieval-augmented generation. Unlike prior work, our pipeline relies only on image input, so it applies to artworks without links to Wikidata or Wikipedia, common in most digitized collections. ArtSeek integrates three components: a late-interaction multimodal retrieval module, a contrastive multitask network predicting artist, genre, style, media, and tags, and an agentic reasoning strategy enabled through in-context examples. Central to this approach is WikiFragments, a dataset of image-text fragments extracted from the entire English Wikipedia to support knowledge-grounded multimodal reasoning. Our multimodal query encoding raises retrieval NDCG@5 from 33.9 to 44.9 over encoding the full image-text query, and fixed-budget token pooling keeps late-interaction search practical over millions of fragments at under 7% of the cost of full multi-vector retrieval. ArtSeek sets a new state of the art in artwork attribute classification, with +8.4% F1 in style classification over GraphCLIP, and gains +7.1 BLEU@1 on ArtPedia captioning. On five knowledge-grounded question answering benchmarks graded by an LLM judge from a different model family, retrieval helps where the answer is documented in our index or lies inside the classifier's label space (+0.19 mean correctness on two of them) and is neutral where it is not; what separates the two regimes is how much of a benchmark our knowledge base covers. The gain transfers to two non-Qwen backbones (+0.22 to +0.28). A blind human study and a qualitative analysis of successes and failures corroborate this picture and expose where retrieval still misleads. Data and code: https://cilab.di.uniba.it/artseek/</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at WACV 2027. 28 pages (10 main + 18 supplementary)</p></details> |
| **[QwenGyre: An Elastic Reinforcement Learning Framework for Training xLong-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33848v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly undertake extreme-long (xlong) horizon tasks, where a single execution can span hours, hundreds of model--environment interactions, and nearly 1M tokens per rollout. Applying online reinforcement learning (RL) to such executions poses two fundamental challenges: (1) severe execution variance and prolonged rollout delays cause massive GPU idling; and (2) complex non-linear branching generates massive trajectory redundancy, crippling training efficiency. To address these, we presents QwenGyre, an end-to-end framework for xlong-horizon online RL. QwenGyre elastically reallocates GPUs between rollout and training without interrupting live executions, while its trajectory processor reconstructs branching histories, scores partial progress, and deduplicates redundant paths to bound training costs. Scaled to our flagship model, Qwen~3.8 2.4T, with 700K tokens per rollout, QwenGyre yields a 6.0% absolute gain on NL2RepoBench (52.5% $\to$ 58.5%) in 48 steps. Across our evaluations on diverse domains of training datasets, QwenGyre delivers up to $1.85\times$ and $1.78\times$ speedups over Colocate and Async, respectively.</p></details> |  |
| **[From DQI to C-DQI: Automatic Codebook Generation and Evaluation for Measuring Computational Discourse Quality Index](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27937v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Effective democratic deliberation is vital for civic engagement and the health of legislative institutions. However, assessing parliamentary discourse quality is traditionally done using expert-scored, theory-based codebooks (CBs), which are rigid, labor-intensive, and prone to biases. While Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a scalable alternative for both schema induction and discourse annotation, ensuring schema quality and mitigating models' inherent bias remains a clear challenge. In this work, we propose a complete protocol for LLM-based codebook generation and evaluation. Starting from the widely accepted discourse quality Index (DQI), we compare top-down (theory-based), bottom-up (data-driven), and hybrid approaches for generating context-adapted schemas. To evaluate these schemas, we define an extrinsic proxy measure, validated by manual annotations, alongside an intrinsic inter-model agreement (IMA) metric, which leverages differing model biases to signal overall reliability. Our experiments on 100 Israeli parliament transcripts reveal that inductive, data-driven schemas achieve higher IMA than top-down methods while maintaining validity, demonstrating their potential for reliable, large-scale analysis of political discourse.</p></details> |  |
| **[Why Do LLMs Accept Generics? We cast LLM evaluation as a multi-agent peer-assessment problem and introduce Agents-as-Peers, a distributed evaluation protocol in which every agent generates tasks, answers the shared task set, and judges the answers of its peers, with no privileged evaluator. In an instantiation with 12 LLMs and 420 agent-generated questions, Agents-as-Peers aggregates 55,440 non-self peer judgments per regime. Most agents assign their own answers higher scores than those answers receive from peer judges; visible identity changes scores for some agents; the first fixed answer slot is associated with scores 0.39 points higher than the shuffled baseline; and judges differ markedly in severity. On labeled benchmarks, peer scores align strongly with accuracy on TruthfulQA (Pearson r=0.904) and GSM8K (r=0.873). On TruthfulQA, the peer-score correlation is substantially higher than the self-score correlation (r=0.538). A benchmark-overlap screen and item-level human audit further characterize the endogenous task set. These results support Agents-as-Peers as a cohort-relative measurement protocol whose ranking is reported together with the biases and rater effects that shape it. Code and dataset: https://github.com/caura-ai/caura-PeerRank</p></details> | <details><summary>Versi...</summary><p>Version 2.0 of PeerRank</p></details> |
| **[APOLO: Automatic Prompt Optimization for Ontology Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34540v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ontology Learning (OL) from text has advanced with the emergence of Large Language Models (LLMs), but it remains challenging due to the limited availability of annotated training data and the difficulty of adapting LLMs to perform OL effectively. | **[Hearsay or Fact? How Framing and Elicitation Shape LLM Probability Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27745v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as judgment assistants and as stand-in participants in psychology experiments, yet little is known about how much their probability estimates move in response to a single new piece of information. We test seven configurations: two local models (Qwen3-8B, OLMo-2-7B; 240 pairs) and five configurations run through agent command-line harnesses (120 pairs), with hypotheses and analysis rules fixed before the formal runs (not publicly registered).
